We can compute D via direct quadruple triangular coefficient maybe compare R table against known sequence for quadrangular numbers. R(n) is number of representations n as sum of 4 triangular numbers with indices≥0. There is a famous theorem: every n is sum of 4 triangular numbers (Gauss), and count r_Δ4(n) has simple formula! Ah! This could give exact R(n) easily and cross-check! Number of representations of n as sum of four triangular numbers may be related to sum of 4 odd squares of 8n+4. Indeed triangular T_a=a(a+1)/2; 8T_a+1=(2a+1)². Sum 4 T_a=n => sum_{i=1}^4 (2a_i+1)² =8n+4. Ordered a_i correspond positive odd roots, and ordered quadruples. There is a known formula: number of representations of 8n+4 as sum of four odd squares. All representations of number ≡4 mod8 by four squares must all odd (since each odd square1 mod8, four gives4). So R(n)=r_4(8n+4)/16? Integer signed ordered count: each a≥0 maps to odd root 2a+1>0, and in signed representations all four coordinates are odd nonzero, each has 2 signs. Thus r_4(8n+4)=16 R(n). Jacobi r4 formula gives R(n)=1/16 * 8 Σ_{d|(8n+4),4∤d} d = (1/2) Σ_{d|(8n+4),4∤d} d. Since 8n+4=4(2n+1), divisors not divisible by4 are divisors of 2n+1 plus 2 times odd divisors? Specifically all d|4m with 4∤d: d odd divisors of m and 2*odd divisors of m. Sum=3σ(m), where m=2n+1 odd. Thus R(n)= (3/2) σ(2n+1)! Wow.
